2014 - Coração Valente

Brazil entered 2014 expecting a party and found an argument instead. The World Cup was finally arriving, unemployment was still low and the social gains of the previous decade remained real, but growth had slowed, inflation was becoming a kitchen-table subject, the June 2013 demonstrations were still hanging over Brasília and Petrobras was turning from national symbol into a word politicians preferred to pronounce carefully.

Dilma Rousseff begins as the favorite, armed with the presidency, Lula and more television time than anyone can reasonably use. Aécio Neves has the largest opposition party, a record in Minas Gerais and twelve years of accumulated PSDB impatience. Eduardo Campos has Pernambuco, the PSB and Marina Silva standing beside him — which is both an extraordinary asset and a useful reminder that most of the country is still learning his name.

The election will move through a World Cup, scandals, television wars, coalition bargaining and an August no campaign memo can prepare for. Pick a candidate, build a strategy and try to discover which promises survive contact with twenty-seven states, eleven parties and one hundred million voters.

Four years ago, Lula pointed at you and asked Brazil to trust him.

They did.

Since then, you have become Brazil's first female president, watched unemployment remain low, presided over an economy that has become substantially less cooperative, survived millions of people taking to the streets in 2013, and discovered that maintaining a nine-party governing coalition is somewhat more complicated than managing an electrical grid.

You remain the favorite. You have the presidency, the largest television coalition in the race, Lula, a popular social-policy legacy and an opposition that has spent most of the last decade trying to decide whether it wants to repeal your party's programs or claim partial authorship of them.

You also have inflation, Petrobras, a World Cup whose political meaning changes every ninety minutes, and an electorate that increasingly seems to agree on only one thing: they would like you to explain yourself. The campaign wants reassurance without complacency, change without admitting anything went wrong, and continuity without making four more years sound like an administrative extension.

Fortunately, you have never been accused of giving short explanations.
